# Stage 1.1: the transaction question, decided before any code

The previous section ended the axis-3 story with one sentence that is a decision, not a measurement:

> The blocker that outlives the types is the transaction. [...] a port must supply snapshot/rollback
> itself. That is code, not a name, which is why no rule was written and why the count on this axis did not
> move.

This section answers that question **before** any code is written, in the order the option space deserves,
and it is written down here first so that the code that follows can be judged against it.

## What the 26.2 bridge actually relies on

Reading `PairedTransactions` and `ForgeSnapshotAdapters` rather than the type list, the dependency is narrower
than "NeoForge transactions":

1. **The journal belongs to the provider, not to the bridge.** `ItemStackHandler` on 26.2 registers its own
   `SnapshotJournal` with the `TransactionContext` it is handed, and reverts itself when that context aborts.
   The bridge never snapshots a native store. `ForgeSnapshotAdapters` exists only for the *legacy Forge* store,
   which has no transaction of its own, and it journals that one on the bridge's side.
2. **The bridge pairs two engines.** `PairedTransactions` opens a NeoForge transaction that is a peer of the
   Fabric transaction the consumer holds, so that closing one closes the other at the same nesting boundary
   (`beforeClose`/`afterClose`, `TransactionTransactionHooks`). No value is ever copied across; the two engines
   just have to agree on when to commit.
3. **Therefore "half-succeeded cross-API insert" means**: a Fabric consumer's transaction did one real write
   into a NeoForge store and then aborted. The NeoForge store's own journal is what takes that write back.

So the port's problem is not "reimplement a transaction engine". It is: **on 1.21.1 the write into a
handler-API store is not journalled by anyone, because there is no context to hand it.**

## The four ways out, and which one is right

**A. One source tree that speaks handlers on both lines.** 26.2 still ships the legacy handler API
(`items.IItemHandler`, `ItemStackHandler`, `ItemHandlerHelper`, `fluids.capability.IFluidHandler`,
`energy.IEnergyStorage` -- javap in `neoforge-runtime.jar`), so a bridge written against handlers would
compile for 26.2 and 1.21.1 both, and the transfer package would not need a second edition at all.

**Rejected, and the reason is the question the stage asked me to answer.** On 26.2 the handler API is not the
same capability as the new API: `Capabilities.Item.BLOCK` is a `BlockCapability<ResourceHandler<ItemResource>,
Direction>` while `Capabilities.ItemHandler.BLOCK` is one over `IItemHandler` (javap both). A 26.2 mod that
registers its storage with the **new** capability would become invisible to this bridge, and therefore to every
Fabric consumer -- the bridge would keep working for old mods and silently stop covering new ones. The
direction that would keep both visible does not exist: 26.2 ships `items.ItemResourceHandlerAdapter`, which
adapts a *handler* **into** the new API. There is no adapter from a `ResourceHandler` back to `IItemHandler` to
pair it with. And the cost is not only conceptual: the transfer suite (`src/transferTest`) pins the
`ResourceHandler` shapes, the journal depths and the notification timing, so the single-tree route would have
to change the behaviour of the line that currently works, in a stage whose hard constraint is that it must not.

**B. Give up rollback on 1.21.1 and record the behaviour difference.** There are two versions of this and both
are worse than they look:

- *Expose nothing that cannot be rolled back.* That is what the 26.2 code already does for unauditable
  providers (`FORGE_HANDLER_NOT_ROLLBACK_SAFE`), and on 1.21.1 the same policy would refuse **every** store,
  because no handler has a journal. The bridge would be a switch that installs and then answers nothing --
  dead code with extra steps.
- *Expose it anyway, and document that abort does not undo.* This is not a smaller contract, it is a broken
  one: a Fabric consumer's `simulate` (which is *defined* as an operation followed by an abort) would really
  mutate the neighbour's inventory, and `ContainerStorage`-style double moves would duplicate or destroy
  items. Documenting a corruption path is not porting it, and the error count would move by an act of
  documentation.

**C. Implement the snapshot ourselves, driven by the transaction that survives.** **This is the answer.**

**D. Delete the transfer component from the 1.21.1 line** (don't overlay the package; the errors go away
because the code does). Rejected without much agonising: it is the only option that would reduce the number by
removing the capability the number is about, and the bridge is the reason this kernel exists. It would also
make `1.3`'s "axis 3 drops" true and worthless.

## Why C is available: the transaction did not disappear, one of its two engines did

Fabric's transfer API is a *mod*, identical on both lines, and 1.21.1's copy has the whole transaction
machinery. Measured by javap against the 1.21.1 measurement class path:

```
net.fabricmc.fabric.api.transfer.v1.transaction.TransactionContext
  openNested() / nestingDepth() / getOpenTransaction(int)
  addCloseCallback(TransactionContext$CloseCallback)
  addOuterCloseCallback(TransactionContext$OuterCloseCallback)

net.fabricmc.fabric.api.transfer.v1.transaction.base.SnapshotParticipant<T>
  abstract T createSnapshot(); abstract void readSnapshot(T); void releaseSnapshot(T);
  void onFinalCommit(); void updateSnapshots(TransactionContext);
  void onClose(TransactionContext, TransactionContext$Result);
  void afterOuterClose(TransactionContext$Result)
```

`SnapshotParticipant` **is** NeoForge's `SnapshotJournal`, under Fabric's name, and it is the protocol Fabric
documents for exactly our case: a storage whose writes must be reversed when the transaction that made them
aborts, at the nesting depth at which they were made. So the port is not "write a transaction engine"; it is
"move the snapshot to the side that still has one".

The mapping, operation by operation:

| 26.2 | 1.21.1 |
|---|---|
| bridge hands the consumer's `TransactionContext` to a native store, whose own journal registers with it | bridge captures a snapshot of the handler store **before** mutating and registers it with the consumer's Fabric `TransactionContext` |
| abort at depth *n* -> the store's journal reverts to its depth-*n* snapshot | abort at depth *n* -> `SnapshotParticipant.onClose` reverts to the depth-*n* snapshot |
| commit -> `onRootCommit` runs the deferred `setChanged()` callbacks once | commit -> `afterOuterClose` runs them once |
| unauditable Forge store -> no view, `FORGE_HANDLER_NOT_ROLLBACK_SAFE` | identical policy, identical issue code |
| a Fabric consumer's `simulate` = open nested, insert, abort | identical (Fabric's own transaction, unchanged) |
| a Forge/Neo consumer's `simulate` (no transaction of its own) = open a scope under the current one, do it, abort or commit | identical, minus the Neo half of the pairing |

Three consequences that are properties of the design, not accidents:

- **`simulate` is never trusted.** On 26.2 a `ResourceHandler` has no simulate flag, so a write is always real
  and always journalled; on 1.21.1 a handler *does* have the flag, and the bridge still does not trust it:
  every mutating call goes through a snapshot. That is strictly stronger than `insertItem(..., true)`.
- **The bridge is now the only transaction there is** for a NeoForge-native store on 1.21.1, because on that
  line a NeoForge store *is* a handler. There is no native journal to defer to, and the code says so instead of
  implying otherwise.
- **What cannot be certified the same way.** 26.2's reversibility certificate is a set of post-transform
  **bytecode fingerprints** (`ForgeTransferShapeAudit.AUDITED`, e.g.
  `net.minecraftforge.items.ItemStackHandler -> be15e7bb...`). Those hashes are of 26.2 bytes and cannot match
  1.21.1's. The 1.21.1 edition therefore certifies by **shape** -- exact class (no subclass can override a
  transfer method), the audited private field layout, and no foreign override of the helpers -- and this
  document says plainly that it is weaker in exactly one respect: a third party that *transforms* the standard
  class itself would not be noticed, where the fingerprint would notice. Producing a 1.21.1 fingerprint set is
  a separate job (it needs a 1.21.1 emit pass) and is **not** done here.

## What genuinely cannot be ported, said now rather than discovered later

The **Forge-consumer direction has no seam on 1.21.1.** `ForgeTransferCapabilityFallback` injects into
`BlockEntity.getCapability(Capability, Direction)`, and that method does not exist on the 1.21.1 merged base:

```
$ javap -cp <1.21.1 measurement class path> net.minecraft.world.level.block.entity.BlockEntity
  ... no getCapability(Capability, Direction) anywhere in the class ...
  implements ... net.minecraftforge.common.extensions.IForgeBlockEntity
$ javap ... net.minecraft.world.level.block.entity.BaseContainerBlockEntity
  public <T> LazyOptional<T> getCapability(Capability<T>, Direction);     <- only here
```

So on 1.21.1 Forge mods' `getCapability` calls cannot be extended by that transformer, and the bridge reports
`FORGE_QUERY_HOOK_MISSING` exactly as 26.2 does when a hook is absent. Two things this does **not** break,
which is why the rest of the axis is still worth porting:

- **Reading** a Forge owner's store needs no hook: it is a direct
  `ICapabilityProvider.getCapability(capability, face)` call on the block entity, which 1.21.1 has.
- The **NeoForge-consumer** direction has its seam: `BlockCapability.getCapability(Level, BlockPos,
  BlockState, BlockEntity, C)` exists on 1.21.1 (javap), which is what `TransferCapabilityFallback` targets.
- The **Fabric-consumer** direction is public API (`BlockApiLookup.registerFallback`), unchanged.

One more API fact that follows from the same javap: `Level.getCapability` does not exist on 1.21.1 either, so
every native query in the 1.21.1 edition goes through `Capabilities.ItemHandler.BLOCK.getCapability(...)`
rather than `level.getCapability(...)`.

## Where the decision is enforced in the code

The 1.21.1 edition of the transfer package is an **overlay**: `src/runtime/mc-line/1.21.1/java/` replaces the
files that name the absent package, through the same `-Pforbric.mcLine` knob that already normalises the
sources -- no second version switch. Files that are already line-neutral (`TransferPrecedence`,
`TransferIssues`, `EnergyUnits`, `ForgeCapabilityWatch`, `KernelFabricHopperStorage`, `package-info`) stay
shared, so the two lines cannot drift apart on the decisions they agree about. The overlay list is 13 files,
exactly the 13 that carry axis-3 errors, and the build fails if an overlay file does not replace a real source
file (so the list cannot rot into a pile of new files nobody deletes).

The internal currency of the 1.21.1 edition is the **NeoForge handler** (`items.IItemHandler`,
`fluids.capability.IFluidHandler`, `energy.IEnergyStorage`), for the same reason the 26.2 edition's currency is
`ResourceHandler`: it is the richest handler API on its own line. Cross-ecosystem paths then compose through
it -- Fabric <-> NeoForge in `NativeTransferAdapters`, Forge <-> NeoForge in `ForgeLegacyFacades` -- instead of
growing an adapter for every pair.

## What stage 1.3 measures, and what it will not prove

`1.3` is a count: axis 3 must fall, cascades must not rise. It is worth writing down in advance that **a lower
count is not evidence that the semantics above are right**. The journal is exercised by the 26.2 transfer tests
only; the 1.21.1 edition of it has never been loaded, and nothing in this stage can change that.

---

# Stage 1.2 and 1.3: the handler-side bridge written, and the axis-3 count closed

Written after the section above, which was written before any code. **Nothing in this section changes that
decision**; it is the code that follows it, the count it produced, and what it still does not prove.

## 1.2: what was written

The 1.21.1 edition of the transfer package is a **source overlay**, not a rename table, because the difference is
a subsystem rather than a spelling. The mechanism reuses the one knob that already existed:

```
forbric-kernel/src/runtime/mc-line/1.21.1/java/net/forbric/kernel/runtime/transfer/*.java   (13 files)
```

`normalizeRuntimeJava` (same task, same `-Pforbric.mcLine`) writes each of those files **verbatim** into the
generated tree, and no rename rule is applied to them: they are already written in 1.21.1's own names, and a rule
that matched inside one would be a guess. Two guards make the list honest instead of a pile:

- every overlay file must replace a **real** file at the same path under `src/runtime/java`; a file that replaces
  nothing fails the build with the two ways out (`doFirst` in `normalizeRuntimeJava`);
- the rename table's "matched nothing" guard still runs against the **pre-overlay** sources, so a table row whose
  only match lived in an overlaid file is still counted, and a genuinely stale row still fails.

The six line-neutral files stay shared, so the two lines cannot drift on the things they agree about:
`TransferPrecedence` (which source answers), `TransferIssues` (the issue codes), `EnergyUnits`, `ForgeCapabilityWatch`,
`KernelFabricHopperStorage`, `package-info`.

**The internal currency is the NeoForge handler** (`items.IItemHandler`, `fluids.capability.IFluidHandler`,
`energy.IEnergyStorage`), for the same reason the 26.2 edition's currency is `ResourceHandler`: it is the richest
handler API on its own line. Cross-ecosystem paths compose through it instead of growing an adapter per pair --
Fabric <-> NeoForge in `NativeTransferAdapters`, Forge <-> NeoForge in `ForgeLegacyFacades`.

The per-file story, in the order the decision implies:

| file | 26.2 | 1.21.1 |
|---|---|---|
| `PairedTransactions` | pairs two engines, journals belong to the stores | the rollback layer: a `SnapshotParticipant` journal base, the scope opener, the invariant registry, the guard hooks. No peer, because there is no second engine |
| `ForgeSnapshotAdapters` | journalled the Forge store only | journals **both** ecosystems' `ItemStackHandler`/`FluidTank`, driven by the consumer's Fabric context; structural certificate instead of a bytecode fingerprint |
| `ForgeEnergyAdapters` | `IEnergyStorage` <-> `EnergyHandler`, journalled on the NeoForge side | `IEnergyStorage` <-> `IEnergyStorage` as a pure type adapter (neither API has a transaction), plus the energy store journal that Team Reborn's transactional consumer needs |
| `NativeTransferAdapters` | `ResourceHandler` <-> `SlottedStorage` | NeoForge handler <-> `SlottedStorage`; `simulate` becomes "a real operation in a nested scope that is aborted", and a write into a handler store is always preceded by a snapshot |
| `LiveTransferEndpoints` | live views over `ResourceHandler` | same views over handlers, and each one can now **name the store it would write**, so the snapshot and the write are the same object |
| `ForgeLegacyFacades` | Forge facades over `ResourceHandler` | Forge <-> NeoForge handler facades; fluids cross through `ForgeFluidMetadata`, which on this line is still a tag <-> components conversion |
| `ForgeFluidMetadata` | Forge tag -> components | unchanged in shape, because 1.21.1 has the SAME split (javap: Forge `hasTag/getTag`, NeoForge `getComponentsPatch`) |
| `TransferResources`, `TransferCodec`, `EnergyAbilities` | resource-typed | stack-typed / handler-typed; `EnergyAbilities` survives only for views whose flags are not the wrapped store's |
| `BlockTransferBridge` | `ResourceHandler` views and `Capabilities.Item/Fluid/Energy` | handler views and `Capabilities.ItemHandler/FluidHandler/EnergyStorage`, `BlockCapability.getCapability` instead of `Level.getCapability`, and the Forge-consumer direction reported dormant |
| `RebornEnergyAdapters`, `RebornEnergyBridge` | Reborn <-> `EnergyHandler` | Reborn <-> `IEnergyStorage`; Reborn's side is the one that keeps a real context, so it is where the journal is installed |

Three behaviours worth naming, because they are the decision showing up in code rather than in prose:

- **No handler is ever asked to simulate.** `insertItem(slot, stack, simulate)` exists on this line; the bridge does
  not use it to decide anything it depends on. Every mutating call is real and snapshot-covered, and the only
  `simulate` flags the bridge honours are the ones a consumer *asks* for (answered with a real operation in a
  nested scope that is then aborted).
- **The Forge-consumer direction is dormant and says so.** `install()` reports `FORGE_QUERY_HOOK_MISSING` because
  `BlockEntity` on this line declares no `getCapability(Capability, Direction)`; `forgeFallback`/`forgeOwnerFirst`
  exist as the ABI the transformer would call and stay gated exactly as 26.2 gates a missing hook. Reading a Forge
  owner's store is *not* affected -- that path never went through the hook.
- **One real gap of the 1.21.1 fluid API is refused, not faked.** `IFluidHandler` can be read per tank but written
  only as a whole handler, so a slotted Fabric write into a **multi-tank** NeoForge handler answers 0 with one
  `FLUID_TANK_UNADDRESSABLE` issue; a single-tank handler (every `FluidTank`) is exact, and the unslotted path works
  for both.

## 1.3: the counts, measured identically, before and after

Same javac (JDK 21, `-Xmaxerrs 10000 --release 21`, locale-pinned), same 151 sources, same procedure with
`extractTransferApis` re-run for the line first. `before` is HEAD (the previous round's tree, re-measured rather
than inherited); `after` is this tree.

| M1 -- energy jar as shipped (class-file 69) | TOTAL | axis 1 | axis 2 | axis 3 | cascades |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| before | **549** | 102 | 117 | 210 | 120 |
| after | **319** | 100 | 111 | 23 | 85 |

| M2 -- energy jar retargeted to class-file 65 | TOTAL | axis 1 | axis 2 | axis 3 | cascades |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| before | **524** | 102 | 117 | 187 | 118 |
| after | **294** | 100 | 111 | 0 | 83 |

The `before` pair reproduces the previous round's numbers exactly (549 and 524), so the two sides are comparable.
On **M2, axis 3 is 0**: every error that named `net.neoforged.neoforge.transfer` is gone. On **M1, axis 3 is 23**,
and those 23 are not the port. They are the energy jar's own unreadability: one `cannot access EnergyStorage` whose
detail is `bad class file: ... class file has wrong version 69.0, should be 65.0`, plus the 22 unresolved
`EnergyStorage` symbols that one diagnostic causes. The 23 fall in exactly the two classes that name a Team Reborn
type (`RebornEnergyAdapters` 10, `RebornEnergyBridge` 13), and the same 23 exist before the port (M1 axis 3 210
minus M2 axis 3 187), which is what makes that reading safe.

**The delta is a set difference of the two transcripts, and it is exact: 188 unique error blocks removed, 0 added,
all 188 inside the 13 overlaid files.** That is the whole case for "the errors went away because the code was
ported", and it is also why the cascades fell rather than rose -- they were *inside* those files, downstream of the
missing types, and went with them:

```
removed  32 BlockTransferBridge       removed  20 ForgeLegacyFacades      removed   9 RebornEnergyAdapters
removed  27 ForgeSnapshotAdapters    removed  17 ForgeEnergyAdapters     removed   6 ForgeFluidMetadata
removed  22 LiveTransferEndpoints    removed  16 TransferResources       removed   5 EnergyAbilities
removed  16 PairedTransactions       removed  13 NativeTransferAdapters  removed   3 RebornEnergyBridge
                                                                         removed   2 TransferCodec
added     0 anywhere
```

Axis 2 fell by 6 and axis 1 by 2 as a side effect, and both are accounted for by the same set difference: the old
`BlockTransferBridge` carried 6 axis-2 errors and the old `ForgeLegacyFacades` 2 axis-1 errors, and those files no
longer exist. Nothing was reclassified into a friendlier bucket; the per-axis columns are the splitter's rules,
unchanged from the previous round.

## 26.2 is still the default and still verified: three checks, reproduced

`JAVA_HOME=C:\Program Files\Java\jdk-25.0.2`, `GRADLE_USER_HOME=<repo>/.gradle-home`, no `-P` property:

1. **Clean build.** `clean jar` -> `BUILD SUCCESSFUL in 15s`, 10 actionable tasks all executed, and the log names
   `src\runtime\java` twice with **zero** mentions of `normalizeRuntimeJava` or `remapTransferApis`.
2. **Fresh runtime compile.** `rm -rf build/classes/java/runtime` then `compileRuntimeJava` -> `BUILD SUCCESSFUL in
   6s`, **295 class files**, and `--dry-run` lists exactly `compileJava`, `processResources`, `classes`,
   `extractTransferApis`, `verifyRebornEnergy`, `compileRuntimeJava`.
3. **The line switch is not sticky.** A 1.21.1 `compileRuntimeJava` printed `13 file(s) REPLACED`, ran
   `normalizeRuntimeJava`, `remapTransferApis` and `verifyRebornEnergy`, then **failed** at `compileRuntimeJava` as
   expected; immediately after, `rm -rf build/classes/java/runtime` and a plain `compileRuntimeJava` ->
   `BUILD SUCCESSFUL in 6s`, 295 class files, neither normalise nor remap task in its graph.

The full suite, reproduced on this tree rather than quoted: **5,627 tests, 79 failures, 842 ignored**, and the 79
decompose exactly as the baseline says -- 77 `java.io.IOException at TempDirectory.java:350` plus exactly two
`AssertionFailedError` (`ForgeRegistrationA2ContractTest.clientGateSeedsF6AndBothScriptsParse`,
`KernelRuntimeClassesTest.everyGameSideClassTheBootSideNamesIsInTheRegistry`). Neither of those two was touched.

One production change beyond the overlay, in `KernelTransferInterop.configure`: the presence probe required
`net/neoforged/neoforge/transfer/ResourceHandler` to decide the transfer layer exists, which is FALSE on 1.21.1, so
the bridge could never install there however well the ported code compiled. It now accepts either native API
(`ResourceHandler` **or** `items/IItemHandler`). On 26.2 the answer is unchanged, because the first disjunct is still
the one 26.2 satisfies.

## What is still not verified

- **The 1.21.1 line still does not compile** (294 errors by M2) and **no 1.21.1 kernel jar has ever been built or
  launched.** Axis 3 is a closed count, not a working transfer subsystem.
- **Not one line of the 1.21.1 edition has ever executed.** The rollback layer is verified by (a) the 1.21.1
  module's `SnapshotParticipant`/`TransactionContext` bytecode, read with `javap -c -p`, (b) compilation against the
  line's own class path, and (c) the fact that the same protocol is exercised on 26.2 by the transfer tests. What is
  NOT verified is that a Fabric abort restores a 1.21.1 `ItemStackHandler` in a running game -- it is an argument
  from the participant contract, not from an executed transfer.
- **The structural certificate is weaker than 26.2's fingerprint** in exactly one way, stated in the class: a third
  party that transforms the standard store class in place would not be noticed. Producing a 1.21.1 fingerprint set
  is a separate job.
- **The Forge-consumer direction is dormant on this line**, by measurement
  (`BlockEntity.getCapability(Capability, Direction)` absent from the 1.21.1 merged base). Whether the kernel should
  grow a different anchor for it is not decided here.
- The `TransferTransactionHooks` transform's two NeoForge anchors are absent on 1.21.1 by construction. The 1.21.1
  edition does not depend on that transform (it takes its commit/abort signal from the public
  `addCloseCallback`), and its `beforeOpen`/`beforeClose`/`afterClose`/`fabricFinal` hooks are correct whether or not
  the transform installs. `neoFinal` is *not* declared by the 1.21.1 edition, because its only caller injects into a
  class that does not exist there.
- The **energy jar** is still a locally retargeted class-69 jar (`-Pforbric.rebornEnergy=`), not a real Java-21
  build; M1 is reported alongside M2 for that reason.
- The **remapped fabric modules are still compile-time only** (yarn mixin selectors), so nothing in
  `build/transfer-api-mc/` may be loaded.

# Stage 2: every remaining axis-2 name adjudicated — 111 -> 95, and only two of them were renames

Written after the stage that asked for exactly this: take the 111 axis-2 errors stage 1 left, decide **for each one**
whether it is a rename or a restructure, take javap evidence for the 1.21.1 side, put the renames in the table and
write down what the restructures need. The order is the stage's order: adjudicate, then rule-or-restructure, then
count.

The headline is the shape of the answer, not the number: **of the 55 distinct absent names on this axis, exactly two
are renames**, and both are *member* renames that the two earlier sweeps could not see. Everything else on the axis is
a restructure, and for most of it the restructure is a design question rather than a mechanical edit.

## The method that found the two renames, and why the two earlier ones could not

The previous rounds swept by **class simple name** (`rename-sweep.py`, word overlap over all 39 jars on the 1.21.1
measurement class path). That sweep is exhaustive for a class that moved, and it is blind to a rename whose owner did
not move — and both renames found here are of that kind:

| 26.2 | 1.21.1 | how it was found |
|---|---|---|
| `ForgeHooks.ingredientBaseCodec(Codec<Ingredient>)` | `ForgeHooks.enhanceIngredientCodec(Codec<Ingredient>)` | same class on both lines, so the class sweep returns nothing; found by listing `ForgeHooks` with `javap -p` on both carriers and diffing the members |
| `…RegisterColorHandlersEvent.BlockTintSources` | `…RegisterColorHandlersEvent.Block` | both nested classes live under the same outer class, and 26.2 renames between its two package trees: `neoforged…$BlockTintSources` on 26.2, `neoforged…$Block` on 1.21.1, and `minecraftforge…$Block` on both |

So this round added a third pass: for every error javac reported as `cannot find symbol: method …` or `method … cannot
be applied to given types`, the class javac had **already resolved** was listed on both carriers and the two member
listings were diffed. That is the only method that can see a member rename, and it is now the missing third of the
sweep rather than a one-off.

Both renames are carried by the same consumer-side check the previous round used. For `ingredientBaseCodec` it is the
nearest thing to a proof available without running the game — the two bodies are the same code:

```
26.2    ForgeHooks.ingredientBaseCodec(Codec<Ingredient>) : Codec<Ingredient>       (javap)
        lambda$ingredientBaseCodec$0 / $1 / $2
1.21.1  ForgeHooks.enhanceIngredientCodec(Codec<Ingredient>) : Codec<Ingredient>   (javap)
        lambda$enhanceIngredientCodec$17 / $18 / $19
        (neither name exists on the other line)
```

`BlockTintSources` is a rename for the **read** path and is not the whole story, which the table now says out loud:
`register(...)` takes `List<BlockTintSource>` on 26.2 and `BlockColor` on 1.21.1, and `BlockTintSource` is a 26.x
vanilla type. The kernel never calls `register`; it casts the event and asks it for the live `BlockColors`. That cast
is right on both lines for a measured reason — `ForgeBlockTintInjector` redirects `BlockColors.createDefault`'s own
post call, so the object is whatever that body constructed, and 1.21.1's body constructs
`RegisterColorHandlersEvent.Block` (`javap -c`). Neither rule would have survived a class-name sweep: one is a member,
the other is a nested class inside an outer class that exists on both lines.

## The mechanism discovery: an overlay steals the rules that only match inside it

This is the one thing in this round that changes how the mechanism has to be used, and it was found by the guard
rather than by reading the code. `normalizeRuntimeJava` writes overlay files **verbatim, with no rule applied**, and
its "a rule that matched nothing fails the build" guard counts matches over the **non-overlaid** tree. So a table row
whose only call site lives in a file that later becomes an overlay starts matching nothing and **fails the build**:

```
> E:\...\src\runtime\mc-line\1.21.1.tsv: 1 rule(s) matched nothing in src/runtime/java:
  [net.minecraftforge.event.entity.living.LivingExperienceDropEvent.BUS].
```

An earlier section of this document says that guard "still runs against the pre-overlay sources, so a table row whose
only match lived in an overlaid file is still counted". **That sentence is wrong** and is corrected here: the guard
skips overlaid files entirely, both for the substitution and for the count. Exactly one rule was affected — the
`LivingExperienceDropEvent.BUS` row, whose single site is `KernelGameServerEvents`, which this round overlays for the
`onExplosionDetonate` arity. The row is commented out in place with that reason written beside it, rather than deleted
silently. The trade is 1 error either way and the overlay is worth more.

## The rules written (2, worth 3 errors)

```
ingredientBaseCodec                                 enhanceIngredientCodec
RegisterColorHandlersEvent.BlockTintSources         RegisterColorHandlersEvent.Block
```

`ingredientBaseCodec` is worth 1 error, `BlockTintSources` 2. Both are whole-token rules with the simple name
**changed**, so neither can fire inside its own target — unlike `Result` and `TriState`, which had to be written
fully-qualified for exactly that reason.

## The restructures written as overlays (6 files, worth 19 errors)

Every one of these is a genuine shape difference in a carrier class, so none of them can be a name. The overlay list
is now 19 files: stage 1's 13 transfer files, plus these six.

| file | 26.2 | 1.21.1 | errors |
|---|---|---|---|
| `KernelForgeCreativeTabs` | `EventHooks`/`ForgeHooks.onCreativeModeTabBuildContents(tab, generator, params, output)` | `(tab, ResourceKey<CreativeModeTab>, generator, params, output)`; `CreativeModeTab` has no `builtInRegistryHolder()`/`getKey()`, so the key is resolved as the game's own `buildContents` resolves it (`javap -c`: `BuiltInRegistries.CREATIVE_MODE_TAB`, `Registry.getResourceKey`, `Optional.orElseThrow`) | 3 |
| `KernelGameClientResourceEvents` | `BakingCompleted.getBakingResult():ModelBakery$BakingResult`; `onModelBake(mm, mb)` | `getModels():Map<ModelResourceLocation,BakedModel>`; `onModelBake(mm, map, mb)` — both sides move together, nothing is converted | 1 |
| `KernelGameDamageEvents` | `onShieldBlock(e, src, amount, stack)`; the event carries the blocking stack; **null** means cancelled (`javap -c`: `CancellableEventBus.post == true -> aconst_null`) | `onShieldBlock(e, src, amount)`; no `getBlockedWith()`; the fired event is returned unconditionally, so the refusal is `isCanceled()` on it | 1 |
| `KernelGameServerEvents` | `onExplosionDetonate(level, explosion, List<BlockPos>, List<Entity>, radius)` | `(level, explosion, List<Entity>, radius)` — there is nowhere to put the block list, and the kernel passes it nowhere else | 1 |
| `KernelForgeDeclarationReaders` | `net.minecraftforge.fml.ModList` is a **static facade**: no `get()`, every member `public static` | an **instance** API: `public static ModList get()`, the same members as instance methods, and **no `getLoadedMods()`** — that list is rebuilt from `forEachModContainer` | 4 (axis 2) + 5 (cascades) |
| `KernelFlowerPots` | `net.neoforged.neoforge.registries.GameData.getFlowerPotBlockTable()` fills and reads NeoForge's `Table<Block,Block,Block>` | `GameData` has no such member on either 1.21.1 carrier; on 1.21.1 the pot mapping is per instance in `FlowerPotBlock.fullPots` (`getFullPotsView()`, and no `getFullPot(Block)`). The 1.21.1 edition owns the same table itself, with the same filler | 3 |

Two details in that table are worth keeping because they are the difference between "ported" and "made to compile":

- **`KernelGameDamageEvents` reads the refusal from a different place.** On 26.2 a cancelled `ShieldBlockEvent` makes
  `onShieldBlock` answer `null`; on 1.21.1 the same call always returns the event and the cancellation is a state flag.
  Substituting nothing and adding the parameter back would have compiled into a call that never unblocks. The edition
  checks `forge == null || forge.isCanceled()`, which is the same branch on both lines — and the cancellation is real
  on 1.21.1, not a dead branch: `ShieldBlockEvent` carries `@net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.Cancelable` there
  (`javap -v`), while 26.2 drops the annotation and implements the `…characteristic.Cancellable` marker interface
  instead, which is exactly why its `onShieldBlock` can answer `null` and 1.21.1's cannot.
- **`KernelFlowerPots` changes who else reads the table.** On 26.2 NeoForge's own code reads it; on 1.21.1 nobody else
  does, because the mapping lives in the block instance. The table's *contents* and *filler* are unchanged, so the
  repair (`rebuildTable`) does the same job; what is stated is that the table is now the kernel's private one.

`KernelForgeCreativeTabs` keeps its four-argument public signature deliberately, because `ForgeCreativeTabsInjector`
redirects the merged call site to it **by descriptor**. That also records a fact the injector will have to face: its
`HOOK_DESC` matches the four-argument carrier call, and 1.21.1's carrier call has five arguments, so the injector's
`REQUIRED` anchor will not find its call site on this line. That is the transformer axis, not the compile axis, and it
is written down rather than papered over.

## The counts, measured identically before and after

Same javac (JDK 21, `-Xmaxerrs 10000 --release 21`, `-J-Duser.language=en -J-Duser.country=US`, UTF-8 stdout),
same 151 sources, same procedure with `extractTransferApis` re-run for the line's own fabric-api first. `before` is
`git stash` of this round's tree — not inherited from the previous section — and both pairs were measured in one
sitting.

| M1 — energy jar as shipped (class-file 69) | TOTAL | axis 1 | axis 2 | axis 3 | cascades |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| before | **319** | 100 | 111 | 23 | 85 |
| after | **297** | 99 | **95** | 23 | 80 |

| M2 — energy jar retargeted to class-file 65, both sides | TOTAL | axis 1 | axis 2 | axis 3 | cascades |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| before | **294** | 100 | 111 | 0 | 83 |
| after | **272** | 99 | **95** | 0 | 78 |

javac's own totals are 319/297 on M1 and 295/273 on M2; the one-error gap on M2 is the same
`cannot access ParametersAreNonnullByDefault` line with no `file:line` that earlier rounds recorded, and it is the
reason the M1 total equals its block count (that diagnostic only appears once the energy jar is readable).

**Axis 2 falls 111 -> 95, cascades fall 83 -> 78, and nothing was moved into a friendlier bucket.**

## The delta is a set difference of the two transcripts: 22 removed, 0 added

Keyed on `(axis, file, message, details)` — i.e. line numbers excluded, because two files keep errors whose line
moved. `removed 22, added 0`, and every removed block is inside a file this round touched:

```
removed   5  KernelForgeDeclarationReaders   (4 axis 2 + 5 cascades collapsed to 5 blocks)
          3  KernelFlowerPots                (axis 2)
          3  KernelForgeCreativeTabs         (axis 2)
          2  KernelForgeBlockColors          (axis 2)   -- table rule
          1  KernelForgeIngredients          (axis 2)   -- table rule
          1  KernelGameClientResourceEvents  (axis 2)
          1  KernelGameDamageEvents          (axis 2)
          1  KernelGameServerEvents          (axis 2)
          1  KernelGameClientResourceEvents  (axis 1)   -- see below
added     0  anywhere
```

(The per-file count above is line-insensitive; the raw line-keyed difference reports 23 removed / 1 added because the
one surviving error in `KernelGameDamageEvents` moved from line 64 to line 79, which a line-keyed multiset counts as a
removal plus an addition. Stage 1 avoided this by overlaying whole files whose every error disappeared.)

**Axis 3 is byte-identical: 0 -> 0 on M2 and 23 -> 23 on M1**, and the 23 are the same 23 (the energy jar's own
unreadability, not the port).

**Axis 1 is 100 -> 99, and that one removal is real and is named.** It is
`incompatible types: ModelBakery cannot be converted to Map<ModelResourceLocation,BakedModel>` at
`KernelGameClientResourceEvents.java:26` — a *consequence* of the axis-2 arity error in the same statement
(`onModelBake(mm, mb)` against 1.21.1's three-argument signature). Fixing the call fixed both, which is why the
acceptance criterion "axis 1 and axis 3 byte-identical" holds for axis 3 and holds for axis 1 **minus that one block
inside a file this round touched**. Nothing was reclassified: the splitter's rules are unchanged, and the axis-3
identity is the check that says so.

## What is still on axis 2, and what each one needs

The remaining 95 errors are 15 restructures, all adjudicated name by name in the table's axis-2 tail with the javap
lines beside them. The four that carry most of the count, and why they were not attempted:

- **The FML transformation view (22).** 26.2's `neoforgespi.transformation` / `fml.classloading.transformation` are
  absent from both 1.21.1 carriers, and modlauncher's classes *are* present — so this is the closest a refusal gets to
  a rename, and it still is not one. Where they live is part of the evidence:
  `cpw.mods.modlauncher.TransformingClassLoader` is in **forge-runtime.jar on both lines** (in neither line's
  `neoforge-runtime.jar`), so the platform is present twice over and what is 26.2-only is the API this kernel uses.
  `cpw.mods.modlauncher.TransformingClassLoader` has the same field name (`classTransformer`) that the kernel writes,
  and nothing under it matches: modlauncher's `ClassTransformer` has
  `transformers`/`pluginHandler`/`transformingClassLoader`/`auditTrail` and no processor set, its constructors are
  package-private inside a named module, and there is no `FMLMixinClassProcessor` to hold the weaver. The 1.21.1
  edition of that view is a port of LibJF's 1.21.1 code path, which is not in this repository.
- **The keyed reload-listener registry (11).** 1.21.1 has the *events* (`RegisterClientReloadListenersEvent`,
  `AddReloadListenerEvent`, both javap'd) and not the keyed/ordered API, and the difference is one level below the
  event name: `EventHooks.onResourceReload` takes three arguments on 26.2 (the third a
  `Map<ListenerKey<?>, PreparableReloadListener>`) and two on 1.21.1. Substituting the event name would leave that call
  with the wrong arity — the lateral move this table exists to refuse.
- **The capability provider (14: twelve `AttachCapabilitiesEvent` inner-class symbols, the `ICapabilityProviderImpl`
  constructor, and `injectCapabilities`' new parameter).** This is the deepest one and it is a design decision, not an
  edit. 1.21.1's `CapabilityProvider` has **neither** abstract method the kernel implements
  (`fireAttachCapabilitiesEvent`, `shouldFireAttachCapabilitiesEvent`), keys the single `AttachCapabilitiesEvent<B>` by
  the provider's **Class**, and its `AsField` constructor takes `(Class<B>, B)` where 26.2's takes `(B, boolean)`.
  So the 1.21.1 edition has to supply `Class<Entity>`/`Class<BlockEntity>`/`Class<Level>` where 26.2's subclasses
  supply the event type, and has to express "do not fire" without an abstract method that no longer exists. Also
  `CapabilityManager.injectCapabilities()` gained a `ModList` parameter. That is the same class of question stage 1.1
  wrote down before writing the transaction, and it is not decided here.
- **`BusGroup` (8: five symbol errors and the three `eventbus.api.bus` package errors).** Its counterpart is
  POSITIVE: 1.21.1 has `BusBuilder`, an expression, and the value has to go into `FMLModContainer`'s
  `eventBusGroup` field, which is typed `BusGroup` on 26.2.

The other eleven names/groups are: the tooltip rework (7), the picture-in-picture pipeline (7), `JarContents`/`IModFile` (6, where
`IModFile.getContents()/getId()` and `IModFile.getSecureJar()/setSecurityStatus` are mutually exclusive and
`IModFileInfo.moduleName()` is abstract on 1.21.1 only), `RecipeContentPayload` (4), `ForgeLayer` (3),
`InFluidPredicate` (3), `AnvilCraftEvent` (2), `DataMapLoader` (2), `TagsUpdatedEvent$ServerDataLoad` (1),
`fireFarmlandTrampleEvent` (1, absent from `ForgeEventFactory` on 1.21.1 entirely), `UnbakedModelParser` (1), plus the
`BreakEvent`-constructor (1) and the two mod-bus `.BUS` sites (2) stage 1 already recorded. That is exactly the 95:
22 + 14 + 11 + 8 + 7 + 7 + 6 + 4 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 1.

## 26.2 still builds and its tests still do not regress

`JAVA_HOME=C:\Program Files\Java\jdk-25.0.2`, `GRADLE_USER_HOME=<repo>/.gradle-home`, no `-P` property:

3. **The line switch is not sticky.** A 1.21.1 `compileRuntimeJava` ran `:normalizeRuntimeJava` (logging

The full suite, reproduced on this tree rather than quoted, and run twice — once mid-round and once on the final
tree: **5,627 tests, 79 failures, 0 errors, 842 skipped** both times (`BUILD FAILED in 13m 13s` / `13m 2s`), counted
from `build/test-results/test/*.xml` rather than from Gradle's console summary
— the console printed `5621 tests, 836 skipped` and the XML says 5627/842, the same counting discrepancy between the

## What is still not verified

- **The 1.21.1 line still does not compile**, and **no 1.21.1 kernel jar has ever been built or launched.** 95 axis-2
  errors (M2) is a count, not a build.
- **Not one line of the six new overlay files has ever executed.** Each is verified by `javap` on both carriers, by
  compilation against the line's own class path, and — where the two lines disagree about a *signal* rather than a
  name — by the consumer's bytecode (`onShieldBlock`'s `aconst_null`, `buildContents`'s `Optional.orElseThrow`). The
  behaviour claims in their javadoc are arguments from those bytecodes, not from an executed event.
- **The two rules are verified by compilation and by `javap` on both lines, not at runtime.** For
  `RegisterColorHandlersEvent.BlockTintSources` that is as far as it can go from here: the `register(...)` payload is
  `List<BlockTintSource>` on 26.2 and `BlockColor` on 1.21.1, so if the kernel ever needs to *register* rather than
  *read*, that is a separate axis-1 restructure.
- **An overlay steals the rules that only match inside it** — measured, and now written into both the table and this
  document. Anything that overlays a file must re-check the table's "matched nothing" guard afterwards; the checker
  used here is a five-line scan for rules with no match outside `src/runtime/mc-line/<line>/java`.
- **`ForgeCreativeTabsInjector`'s anchor will not match on 1.21.1**: its `HOOK_DESC` is the four-argument carrier
  call and 1.21.1's carrier call has five. The runtime class keeps the four-argument signature so the contract is
  unchanged, but the injector itself is transformer-axis work and is not touched here.
- **`KernelFlowerPots`'s table is now the kernel's private one.** Its contents and its filler are the same, and
  nothing else in this tree reads it — but on 26.2 NeoForge's own code reads its table, and on 1.21.1 there is no
  such second reader. That is a difference in who can observe the table, stated rather than glossed.
- The **energy jar** is still a locally retargeted class-69 jar passed with `-Pforbric.rebornEnergy=`; M1 is reported
  alongside M2 for that reason. The **remapped fabric modules are still compile-time only**; the string-valued mixin
  selectors are still yarn names and nothing in `build/transfer-api-mc/` may be loaded.
- The **axis splitter's boundary is a reconstruction**, as before: the totals and the set difference are exact; the
  per-axis columns are this round's rules, unchanged from the previous round.

# Stage 3: `BusGroup` (8) ported as three overlays, and the capability provider REFUSED on a measured capability-model change

Two groups in this round, in that order. Group A is a port and its count falls. Group B is a decision and its
count deliberately does not: the 14 errors are the runtime half of a protocol whose other half has no 1.21.1
edition, and the measurement that settles it is in the merged base's own manifest.

## Group A — `BusGroup` has a counterpart, and it is not a `BusGroup`

`javap` on both carriers (`run/forge-runtime/forge-runtime.jar` for 26.2,
`.dev/staged-1211/run/forge-runtime/forge-runtime.jar` for 1.21.1) says the two lines ship **different eventbus
generations**, and the API the kernel calls exists on exactly one side each:

| | 26.2 (EventBus 7) | 1.21.1 (EventBus 6) |
|---|---|---|
| `net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.bus.BusGroup` | **present**: `DEFAULT`, `create(String)`, `create(String, Class)`, `name()`, `startup()`, `shutdown()`, `dispose()`, `trim()`, `register(Lookup, Class/Object)`, `unregister(Collection)` | **absent** — the whole `api.bus` package is four classes (`BusGroup`, `CancellableEventBus`, `EventBus`, `package-info`) and `api.bus.BusGroup` does not resolve |
| `net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.BusBuilder` | **absent** on the 26.2 carrier | **present**, and it is an *expression*: `builder()` → `markerType(Class)` → `build(): IEventBus`, plus `setTrackPhases`, `setExceptionHandler`, `useModLauncher`, `startShutdown`, `checkTypesOnDispatch` |
| `net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.IEventBus` | **absent** | **present**: `register`, `addListener`, `post(Event)`, `fire(T)`, `start()`, `shutdown()` |
| `FMLModContainer` bus state | `private final BusGroup eventBusGroup;` + `public BusGroup getModBusGroup()` | `private final IEventBus eventBus;` + `public IEventBus getEventBus()` |
| `RegisterEvent.getBus(...)` | `public static EventBus<RegisterEvent> getBus(BusGroup)` | **no such member**, and `RegisterEvent extends net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.Event` instead of a child of `net.minecraftforge.eventbus.internal.Event` |

Three consequences, and they are why this group is three overlay files rather than three table rows:

1. **The type changes, and so does the field that must receive it.** The kernel writes that field *by name*
   through `UnsafeHacks`, so javac checks the type and nothing checks the name:
   `uset(FMLModContainer.class, "eventBusGroup", …)` is a `NoSuchFieldException` at runtime on 1.21.1 that the
   compiler cannot see. `"eventBusGroup"` → `"eventBus"` is part of the port, not a tidy-up.
2. **The creation form changes.** `BusGroup.create("modBusFor" + modId, IModBusEvent.class)` is a static factory
   that takes a NAME; 1.21.1's is `BusBuilder.builder()…build()`, and `BusBuilder` has no name parameter.
3. **The post form changes.** 26.2 routes each event class to its own bus through a per-event static
   `X.getBus(group)`; 1.21.1 posts to its single bus directly. Substituting the type and leaving `.getBus(group)`
   does not compile — and the group is eight *reported* errors, not eight broken lines: `javac` suppresses the seven
   `getBus` call sites because the argument's type is already erroneous (measured — the `before` transcript has no
   error on any of those seven lines, only on the import, the signature and the cast), so `KernelForgeSetup` costs
   3 reported errors for 10 changed lines. The compile count understates this group's work by seven.

What 1.21.1's own loader does with that builder was read out of its bytecode rather than guessed
(`javap -c net.minecraftforge.fml.javafmlmod.FMLModContainer`, the constructor): `BusBuilder.builder()` →
`setExceptionHandler(<invokedynamic>)` → `setTrackPhases(false)` → `markerType(IModBusEvent.class)` →
`useModLauncher()` → `build()` → `putfield eventBus:IEventBus`, with no `start()` anywhere in the constructor.

### The port

Three files are overlays — `KernelForgeContainers`, `KernelForgeSetup`, `KernelForgeRegistries` — because every
difference above is an expression or a type, never a spelling. **No rename-table row was added**, so the "an
overlay steals the rules that only match inside it" trap could not fire; that was checked rather than assumed:
`build/x11/overlay-rules.py` reproduces `normalizeRuntimeJava`'s guard (whole-token, longest-rule-first, overlay
files skipped) over the 47 existing rules and reported **0 orphaned by the 22-file overlay set**, before the files
were written and again after.

- `KernelForgeContainers` — `IEventBus` instead of `BusGroup`; the bus is built by a private `modBus()` that
  carries the disassembly above, with **two builder calls deliberately not reproduced**, each named in its javadoc:
  `setExceptionHandler` (the carrier passes its own `onEventFailed`, which logs through `FMLModContainer`'s
  markers; the bus's own default handler — `EventBus` implements `IEventExceptionHandler` — is what a builder that
  sets none gets) and `useModLauncher()` (that flag selects `ModLauncherFactory`, whose `createWrapper` **enqueues**
  the generated listener class for modlauncher's transformer instead of defining it, and
  `ModLauncherFactory.processWrapperClass` is the only drain; this kernel's loader is not that pipeline, so the
  queue would never drain and no listener would materialise, while the default `ClassLoaderFactory` defines the
  wrapper itself). The field write becomes `"eventBus"`; `startup()` becomes `((IEventBus) bus).start()`.
- `KernelForgeSetup` — the seven `X.getBus(group).post(new X(container, stage))` become `bus.post(new X(…))`. That
  is legal on this line for a checked reason: on 1.21.1 every lifecycle event reaches `Event` through
  `ModLifecycleEvent extends net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.Event`, which is the base class `IEventBus.post`
  requires — and it is a *difference*, not a detail: on 26.2 `ModLifecycleEvent` does not extend `Event` at all
  (`javap` both).
- `KernelForgeRegistries` — the single `RegisterEvent.getBus((BusGroup) busGroup).post(event)` becomes
  `((IEventBus) busGroup).post(event)`. Nothing stands in for the missing per-event lookup, because on this line
  there is nothing to look up.

**Two facts the port states rather than translates.** First, `startup()` on 26.2 is not the "no event dispatches
before this" gate the kernel's javadoc claimed: `BusGroupImpl.startup` iterates the group's buses into
`AbstractEventBusImpl.startup`, whose body is `shutdownFlag.compareAndSet(true, false)` plus an invoker re-arm, and
1.21.1's `IEventBus.start()` is the same idea one generation smaller (`shutdown = false`, and `post` answers false
while it is set). A freshly created bus of either generation dispatches as soon as it exists. The claim was wrong
on 26.2 too, so it was corrected instead of translated. Second, a field this kernel has never had to write on 26.2
exists on 1.21.1: `ModContainer.configHandler` is `protected Optional<Consumer<IConfigEvent>>` there, `ModContainer`'s
own (skipped) constructor writes `Optional.empty()` into it, and `dispatchConfigEvent` reads it with `orElse(null)`
and dereferences the result (`javap -c`), so a null field is an NPE the moment anything dispatches a config event.
The overlay restores `Optional.empty()` — the value the skipped constructor would have left, so a reconstruction and
not a handler — beside the four fields the shared file already restores for the same reason.

**One thing is lost and is written into the code:** 26.2 names each group (`"modBusFor" + modId`) and the name is
what its diagnostics print; `BusBuilder` takes no name, so on 1.21.1 a mod bus is identifiable only by the kernel's
own log lines.

---

## Group B — the capability provider: REFUSED, because the 14 errors are half a protocol and the other half names a class that does not exist on this line

### What was measured

| | 26.2 | 1.21.1 |
|---|---|---|
| `CapabilityProvider` abstract methods | `fireAttachCapabilitiesEvent(B)` and `shouldFireAttachCapabilitiesEvent()`, both `protected abstract` — this is where the kernel's three subclasses plug in | **neither**; `gatherCapabilities()` is `protected final` and routes to a `private doGatherCapabilities` that builds the event itself |
| how the event is fired | the subclass returns it | `doGatherCapabilities` → `ForgeEventFactory.gatherCapabilities(baseClass, provider, provider)` → `new AttachCapabilitiesEvent<>(baseClass, provider)` → `post(event)` on the global bus |
| how it is keyed | five nested event classes — `AttachCapabilitiesEvent.{Entities,BlockEntities,Levels,ItemStacks,LevelChunks}`, each with its own static `BUS` | **one** `AttachCapabilitiesEvent<T> extends GenericEvent<T>`, constructed with `(Class<T>, T)`; `AttachCapabilitiesEvent` has no nested classes at all |
| the provider itself | `CapabilityProvider` **plus five nested provider classes** — `$Entities`, `$BlockEntities`, `$Levels`, `$ItemStacks`, `$LevelChunks` | `CapabilityProvider` **only** — no nested provider classes exist |
| `AsField` | `(B)`, `(B, boolean)` | `(Class<B>, B)`, `(Class<B>, B, boolean)` |
| serialisation | `serializeCaps(ValueOutput)` **and** `serializeCaps(HolderLookup.Provider)` | `serializeCaps(HolderLookup.Provider)` only — `ValueInput`/`ValueOutput` do not exist on this line |
| `CapabilityManager.injectCapabilities` | `injectCapabilities()` | `injectCapabilities(ModList)` |
| does `getCapability` auto-gather? | — | **no**: `getCapabilities()` null or `valid` false ⇒ `LazyOptional.empty()` (`javap -c`), which is what makes "never initialise" a usable inert state |

### The measurement that settles it: the merged base's own manifest

Both merged bases ship `META-INF/forbric/required-ancestor-compositions.tsv`, and `ForbricClassLoader.define`
runs every definition through `RequiredAncestorCompositions.verify`, which throws `LinkageError` when no registered
`AncestorComposition` proves the class's final bytes. The two files, read out of the jars:

```
26.2    Entity       AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider$Entities
        Level        AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider$Levels
        BlockEntity  AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider$BlockEntities

1.21.1  Entity       AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider
        EnderDragonPart  PartEntity     PartEntity          (neoforged -> minecraftforge, a separate row)
        Level        AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider
        BlockEntity  AttachmentHolder   CapabilityProvider
```

**That is the capability-model change, in one table.** 26.2 removed a *different provider class per root*, and each
of those classes is exactly the thing that carries a per-root `AttachCapabilitiesEvent` subclass. 1.21.1 removed
*one class* for all three roots and parameterises it with a `Class` literal. The kernel's
`KernelForgeCapabilities.Entities/$BlockEntities/$Levels` are not three spellings of one thing on this line — there
is nothing per-root for them to return, and the only thing that distinguishes the three is the argument.

And the install half cannot be ported by editing the runtime class, because its preconditions are 26.2 classes.
`ForgeCapabilityProtocol.protocol(removed, …)` requires, for the removed provider: `super == CapabilityProvider`,
**no fields**, and a constructor whose token stream is `[v25:0, o3, m183:CapabilityProvider.<init>(Z)V, o177]`; and
for `AsField`, a constructor `<init>(Lnet/minecraftforge/common/capabilities/ICapabilityProviderImpl;Z)V`. On
1.21.1 the removed provider for all three roots **is** `CapabilityProvider`, whose superclass is `java/lang/Object`
and which has eight fields, and `AsField`'s constructor is `(Class, ICapabilityProviderImpl)` — so `protocol()`
returns false on its first `&&`, `certificate()` returns null, `proves()` can never be true, and the loader throws
`LinkageError` on `Entity`, `Level` and `BlockEntity`. That is a **launch blocker on 1.21.1 that no compile-axis
work removes**, and it is the same subsystem as these 14 errors.

### The four routes, and why the first three are worse

- **PORT THE RUNTIME HALF ONLY (overlay the file: Class-keyed `AsField`, no overrides, `injectCapabilities(ModList.get())`,
  `CompoundTag` save/load).** This is what the compile count wants, and it is the route that was refused. It would
  remove **29** errors, not 14: `KernelForgeCapabilities.java` also carries 6 axis-1 `ValueInput`/`ValueOutput`
  symbols and 9 cascades (six "method does not override or implement a method from a supertype", one
  `getAllScanData` static-context error — the same instance-API `ModList` change stage 2 made — and the two
  `ValueInput`/`ValueOutput` import failures folded in), and an overlaid file has to compile whole. What it would
  produce is a correct class that **nothing constructs**, because the one part of the subsystem that installs it
  still cannot run on this line. The count would fall while the blocker stayed exactly where it is. That is the
  shape stage 1.1 refused twice: "a switch that installs and answers nothing" is not a smaller contract, it is a
  broken one — and here it is worse than a switch, because a class that compiles reads as ported.
- **KEEP THE THREE SUBCLASSES AND GIVE THEM `Class` ARGUMENTS WITHOUT TOUCHING THE PROTOCOL.** Same objection one
  level down: the three classes would exist to pass `Entity.class`/`BlockEntity.class`/`Level.class` and nothing
  else, while the protocol that is supposed to fingerprint them can never see them.
- **REWRITE THE COMPOSITION TO THE 1.21.1 SHAPE HERE.** This is the only route that would actually work, and it is
  not this group: it needs a second certificate (one-provider shape, `AsField(Class,B)` tokens,
  `$Composed.<init>(Class, Object)`), new delegate descriptors for `saveAdditional(CompoundTag,
  HolderLookup.Provider)` / `saveWithoutId(CompoundTag)` instead of the `ValueOutput` seams, and a new set of
  injection anchors — all boot-side transformer work, with its own tests (`ForgeCapabilityProtocolTest`,
  `ForgeCapabilityCompositionTransformerTest`), and none of it in the 14.
- **DO NOTHING AND SAY WHY (chosen).** The 14 errors stay, the total stays where Group A left it, and the next
  stage gets a decision with the evidence attached instead of 27 errors' worth of dead code.

### What a 1.21.1 edition needs, so the next stage does not have to re-measure it

1. **A protocol certificate for the one-provider shape.** `removed` is `net/minecraftforge/common/capabilities/CapabilityProvider`
   for all three roots (from the manifest), with `AsField(Class<B>, B)` — so the fingerprint must be taken from
   `CapabilityProvider`'s own field/constructor layout plus that constructor, not from a subclass that has none.
2. **Three accessors that differ only in a `Class` literal**, and `Inert` expressed as NEVER INITIALISING rather
   than as an override answering false: 1.21.1's `getCapability` does not auto-gather, so an un-initialised
   provider is empty forever, which is the same end state 26.2 reaches by marking the provider initialised with a
   null dispatcher.
3. **A save/load seam in `CompoundTag`**, because this line's roots save through
   `saveAdditional(CompoundTag, HolderLookup.Provider)` and `saveWithoutId(CompoundTag)` and have no
   `ValueInput`/`ValueOutput` at all (`serializeCaps(ValueOutput)` does not exist on this carrier; only the
   `HolderLookup.Provider` overload does).
4. **`injectCapabilities(ModList.get())`** — an instance API on this line, the same change stage 2 made in
   `KernelForgeDeclarationReaders`.
5. **The listener shape, which is the mod-facing half of the model change:** a mod subscribes to
   `AttachCapabilitiesEvent<Entity>` and the bus filters GENERICALLY, against one global bus, instead of naming
   `AttachCapabilitiesEvent.Entities` and a per-type `BUS`. There is no event class on this line to name, so any
   kernel-side code that asks "are there listeners for the entity event" has to ask the generic question instead.

**Nothing in Group B was written.** The refusals above are the deliverable, and the count below reflects it.

---

Keyed on `(axis, file, message, details)`, line numbers excluded. `removed 9, added 0`, and the result is identical

rule — its `symbol:` pattern matches `class|interface|enum` only, and its `location: class KernelForgeContainers`

## What is still not verified

- **The 1.21.1 line still does not compile**, and **no 1.21.1 kernel jar has ever been built or launched.** 87

- **Not one line of this stage's three overlays has ever executed.** Each is verified by `javap` on both carriers,
  by `javac` against the line's own class path, and by the carrier's own bytecode for the two claims that are
  behavioural rather than structural (`startup()` is a resurrection and not a gate, on both lines;
  `dispatchConfigEvent` dereferences `configHandler`'s `orElse(null)`). The bus the kernel now creates on 1.21.1 is
  a `BusBuilder` expression that no process has ever run.
- **The `configHandler` write is compile-verified only.** `Optional.empty()` is the value `ModContainer`'s own
  constructor writes, so it restores rather than invents — but nothing has dispatched a config event through it.
- **The two builder calls left out of `modBus()` are a judgement, and they are named in the code.** Dropping
  `useModLauncher()` is argued from `ModLauncherFactory.createWrapper` enqueueing into a queue only

  preference: the composition protocol's preconditions are 26.2 classes, so the runtime half alone would be dead
  code and the loader would still refuse to define `Entity`, `Level` and `BlockEntity` on this line
  (`RequiredAncestorCompositions.verify` → `LinkageError`). **That is a launch blocker on 1.21.1 that no
  compile-axis work removes**, and it is bigger than the 14 errors: it means the next stage for this line should be
  the 1.21.1 edition of `ForgeCapabilityProtocol`/`ForgeCapabilityCompositionTransformer`, not more error shaving.
  The five things that edition needs are listed above; none of them was attempted.
- **The transformer half of the capability subsystem was read, not run.** `protocol()` returning false on 1.21.1 is

  `!fields.isEmpty()`) applied to `javap` output; the `LinkageError` that follows is read out of
  `RequiredAncestorCompositions.verify`, not observed.

  alongside M2 for that reason. The **remapped fabric modules are still compile-time only**, so nothing in
