# Stage 18: the stagedRoot experiment was run, and its prediction was wrong

Stage 14 (above, "the suite number was wrong, and it was wrong silently") predicted that

    gradlew.bat --no-daemon test -Pforbric.stagedRoot=.dev/staged/run

would produce 5,635 / 79 / 842, and that 846 - 842 = 4 would be "exactly those four canary tests".
**The experiment was run. The prediction was wrong, and wrong in a way worth recording.**

## What actually happened

    5,635 tests / 80 failures / 0 errors / 806 skipped

against a documented baseline of 5,635 / 79 / 0 / 842. So 40 fewer skips and 2 more failures, not
4 and 1.

The canaries DID start running, which is the part the diagnosis got right:

    ForgeClientCanaryTest        tests=4  skipped=0  failures=0   (was 4 skipped)
    WorldgenCanaryDataTest       tests=1  skipped=0  failures=0   (was 1 skipped)
    GateWorldgenContractTest     tests=1  skipped=0  failures=1   (was 1 skipped)

But `TestFixtures.stagedRoot()` is not a canary-specific knob. It is read by a large number of tests,
and `-Pforbric.stagedRoot` moves ALL of them at once. The other 36 newly-unskipped tests were reading
`merged-base/patched-mc-merged-26.2.jar` and friends out of `.dev/staged/run` — the 1.21.1 staging
area — and getting artifacts that are not what the 26.2 tests expect. That produced:

    MergedBaseInteropAdoptionTest.everyCallTheBaseMakesUnderTheOldNameIsAdoptedAndResolves
      "content drift: this base names no old-loader hook — nothing to adopt"

which reads like a 26.2 regression and is not one. The test resolves
`TestFixtures.stagedRoot().resolve("merged-base/patched-mc-merged-26.2.jar")` (line 56), so pointing
stagedRoot at the 1.21.1 staging area handed it the wrong jar. **The artifact that carries the
first-generation loader hook was never touched; the test was simply pointed at a directory that does
not hold it.**

## So the fourth hazard caught the stage that documented it

Stage 14 named this exact shape — "a directory shared between two lines decides a number and nothing
in the output says which was used" — and then the next experiment on it was itself a victim: one
property, forty tests changed behaviour, and the failure message named a different cause entirely.
That is the hazard working as designed, which is the argument for writing it down rather than fixing
the symptom.

## The corrected baseline, which was never measured precisely

The acceptance line in this document says "79 failures = 77 Windows TempDir IOExceptions plus exactly
two known real failures". Measured, from the XML of the run with the moved stagedRoot:

    76 failures   java.io.IOException "Failed to delete temp directory ... junit<N>"
     1 failure    ForgeRegistrationA2ContractTest.clientGateSeedsF6AndBothScriptsParse   (known)
     1 failure    KernelRuntimeClassesTest.everyGameSideClassTheBootSideNamesIsInTheRegistry  (known)
     1 failure    GateWorldgenContractTest   (canary, newly running, fails)
     1 failure    MergedBaseInteropAdoptionTest   (wrong stagedRoot, see above)

So "77 TempDir" has been 76 in every run this document can cite, and the two "known real" ones are
two test METHODS that expand into more than two XML testcases in some classes. The 79 figure has been
carried forward from an earlier measurement and quoted rather than re-derived. **A baseline that is
quoted instead of measured is exactly the failure mode this document keeps recording.**

## What to do instead, if this is ever revisited

Do not move `stagedRoot` to run the canaries. Place the two canary jars where the default stagedRoot
already looks for them:

    forbric-kernel/run/forge-runtime/forbriclive.jar        (copy from .dev/staged/run/...)
    forbric-kernel/run/neoforge-runtime/forbricneolive.jar  (copy from .dev/staged/run/...)

That is additive, it leaves every other stagedRoot consumer reading what it read before, and it is
reversible by deleting two files. Moving the root is neither.

# Stage 19: the strict re-scan — 22 types are genuinely absent

Stage 16 counted 37 absent imports out of 228 and subtracted the 14 `neoforge.transfer.*` ones the
stage-1 bridge satisfies, arriving at 23. That subtraction used exact FQN matching only, which is both
too narrow and too broad: too narrow because `net.minecraftforge.A.B` can appear on 1.21.1 as
`net.neoforged.neoforge.A.B` or as `A$B`, and too broad because a same-simple-name hit is not the same
class.

The strict re-scan applies four lookups to every absent name and reports which one hit:

    1. exact FQN
    2. package rewrite: net.minecraftforge.X -> net.neoforged.neoforge.X
    3. inner-class form: the last dot becomes $
    4. both rewrites combined

and falls back to a simple-name search only as a REPORTED candidate, never as a resolution.

## Result

    37 absent
    14 satisfied by the stage-1 transfer bridge
    23 non-transfer absent
     1 resolved by lookup 3: FluidInteractionRegistry.InteractionInformation
                             -> FluidInteractionRegistry$InteractionInformation  (same class)
    22 genuinely absent after all four lookups

The one real find is the shape that hides: the source writes
`net.minecraftforge.fluids.FluidInteractionRegistry.InteractionInformation`, and 1.21.1 has
`net.neoforged.neoforge.fluids.FluidInteractionRegistry$InteractionInformation`. TWO differences at once
— one package segment and one inner-class separator. An import check matching dotted names verbatim
reports it absent; it is present. It belongs in the rename table.

## False candidates, recorded so nobody promotes them

`cpw.mods.modlauncher.TransformingClassLoader` and `cpw.mods.modlauncher.ClassTransformer` share simple
names with two missing names. They are modlauncher's own classes, present on BOTH lines inside
forge-runtime.jar. The missing names are FML's wrappers around them — the layer
`neoforgespi.transformation` would have provided and does not.

`net.minecraft.server.packs.resources.Resource` shares a simple name with the missing
`neoforged.neoforge.transfer.resource.Resource`. A vanilla resource-pack handle and a NeoForge transfer
resource are unrelated types sharing a word. This is why a simple-name search must never resolve.

`AnvilUpdateEvent` is NOT `AnvilCraftEvent`. An earlier pass in this session reported that as a rename
on the strength of a fuzzy match; the strict scan does not find it, and that conclusion is retracted.

## The 22

    (The 8 transfer names — EnergyHandler, FluidResource, ResourceHandler, ItemResource,
     VanillaContainerWrapper, TransactionContext, SnapshotJournal, Resource — are NOT among these
     22. The stage-1 bridge satisfies them, so they are counted in the 14 above, not here.)

    reload-listener registry                        6  ListenerKey, NeoForgeReloadListeners,
                                                      VanillaClientListeners, VanillaServerListeners,
                                                      AddClientReloadListenersEvent,
                                                      AddServerReloadListenersEvent
    class transformation                            5  ClassProcessor, ClassProcessorIds,
                                                      ProcessorName, ClassProcessorSet,
                                                      FMLMixinClassProcessor
    NeoForge tooltips                               4  TooltipAppender, TooltipLocation,
                                                      ItemTooltipHandler, RegisterTooltipAppendersEvent
    picture-in-picture                              2  PictureInPictureRendererRegistration,
                                                      RegisterPictureInPictureRendererEvent
    the 26.x Gui split                              1  ForgeLayer
    singles                                         3  RecipeContentPayload, UnbakedModelParser,
                                                      InFluidPredicate

`Result` is the one whose rewrite has a named destination: `net.minecraftforge.common.util.Result` is
absent, but `net.minecraftforge.eventbus.api.Event$Result` exists and `Event.setResult(Event.Result)`
exists while `PlayerInteractEvent.setUseBlock(...)` does NOT. So it is
`common.util.Result` -> `Event.Result` plus `setUseBlock/setUseItem` -> `setResult`, and the behaviour
changes: `setUseBlock` scopes the decision to the block, `setResult` to the whole event. That difference
must be written down rather than absorbed.

# Stage 20: the overlay set already covers 5 of the absent types — the real count is 17, not 22

Stage 19 arrived at 22 genuinely absent types by scanning `src/runtime/java`. That scan has a hole: it
reads the SOURCE, not the tree the compiler actually sees. `normalizeRuntimeJava` overwrites 37 files
with mc-line overlays, and an overlay replaces a whole file, so a missing import in a file that has an
overlay never reaches javac.

Measured, both directions:

    src/runtime/java                    imports 37 absent Forge/NeoForge types
    src/runtime/mc-line/1.21.1/java      imports 0 absent ones
    19 source files carry the 32 non-transfer absences, 6 of which have an overlay

The 5 types fully covered by overlays — that is, every file importing them is overlaid:

    BusGroup                       KernelForgeContainers, KernelForgeRegistries, KernelForgeSetup
    JarContents                    KernelModFile                    (stage 13's overlay uses SecureJar)
    NeoForgeReloadListeners        KernelNeoDataMapWatch            (stage 9)
    AddServerReloadListenersEvent  KernelNeoDataMapWatch            (stage 9)
    AddClientReloadListenersEvent  KernelGameClientReload           (stage 8)

## The corrected number

    23 distinct non-transfer absent types
     5 already covered because every importing file is overlaid
     1 recovered by the stage-19 inner-class lookup (InteractionInformation)
    17 genuinely absent in the tree the compiler sees

That 17 is the real remaining work. Not 161 compile errors — those are a symptom that stops resolving
once an unrelated statement in the same file fails — and not 22 types, which counted source rather than
compiled input.

## Why this matters for the estimate

Stage 7 estimated 8-20 stages to a compilable jar from an error count. Stage 15 pointed out that count
mixes two kinds of work. This stage adds a third correction: even the TYPE count was inflated, because
6 of the 19 files are already overlaid and their absences are already answered.

So the honest partition of the 17:

    covered by an existing overlay's decision      0   already done
    recoverable by a rename-table row              0   InteractionInformation is in (stage 19)
    needs a new subsystem edition                 11   reload 4, tooltips 4, pip 2, Gui split 1
    needs a one-file decision                      6   TransformerView 5, RecipeSync, ModelFormats,
                                                       FluidBreathing, ClientReloadNames,
                                                       ServerReloadNames, ForgeOverlayLayers,
                                                       ForgeReload, GameWorldEvents, GameBlockEvents

The 11 subsystem ones are the ones with no substrate at all. The 6 single-file ones each need a small
decision that has not been made.

## How to re-derive this

Do not scan `src/runtime/java` alone. The overlay set is a second, larger input:

    # what the compiler sees for a given file
    if src/runtime/mc-line/<line>/java/<path> exists, that file wins, else src/runtime/java/<path>

and every overlay is written against the target line's carriers, so an overlay's import list is the
authoritative one for that file. Stage 13's KernelModFile overlay is the clearest example: the source
imports `net.neoforged.fml.jarcontents.JarContents`, the overlay imports
`cpw.mods.jarhandling.SecureJar`, and only `JarContents` shows up in the source scan.
## CORRECTION to the count above: it is 22, not 17

The "17" and the partitiby breakdown in the section above were computed by hand and are WRONG. A script
that applies the overlay rule file by file gives:

    non-transfer absences in src/runtime/java                     23 distinct types
    in files that HAVE an overlay  (so the absence never reaches javac)
        BusGroup x3 files, JarContents, NeoForgeReloadListeners,
        AddServerReloadListenersEvent, AddClientReloadListenersEvent     5 types
    in files with NO overlay                                             23 distinct types
    minus InteractionInformation, resolved by the stage-19 TSV row        1 type
    ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    genuinely remaining                                                    22 types

The 23 in uncovered files, measured:

     7  KernelFmlTransformerView   ClassProcessorSet, ClassTransformer,
                                   TransformingClassLoader, FMLMixinClassProcessor,
                                   ClassProcessor, ClassProcessorIds, ProcessorName
     4  KernelNeoTooltips          ItemTooltipHandler, TooltipAppender, TooltipLocation,
                                   RegisterTooltipAppendersEvent
     2  KernelForgePipRenderers    RegisterPictureInPictureRendererEvent,
                                   PictureInPictureRendererRegistration
     2  KernelGameBlockEvents      Result, BreakBlockEvent
     2  KernelGameWorldEvents      Result, AnvilCraftEvent
     1  KernelClientReloadNames    VanillaClientListeners
     1  KernelFluidBreathing       InFluidPredicate
     1  KernelForgeOverlayLayers   ForgeLayer
     1  KernelForgeReload          ListenerKey
     1  KernelModelFormats         UnbakedModelParser
     1  KernelRecipeSync           RecipeContentPayload
     1  KernelServerReloadNames    VanillaServerListeners

Note that `Result` and `BusGroup` appear in more than one file, which is why 24 occurrences reduce to 23
distinct types, and why subtracting the 5 overlay-covered types twice (once as occurrences, once as
types) produced the wrong 17.

The lesson is the one this document keeps re-learning: a count computed by hand while writing prose is a
count that has not been measured. Every number above came from a script that walks the source tree,
applies the overlay rule, and indexes the three jars.

# Stage 21: the measurement was restored, and it says 180 — with axis 3 at zero

For the first time in this port the 1.21.1 measurement ran end to end under the documented M2 discipline
and produced a number that is comparable to itself:

    TOTAL 180   (javac says 181 errors)
      axis1-minecraft        73
      axis2-forge-neoforge   91
      axis3-fabric-transfer   0
      unattributed cascades  16
    transcript build/x11/measure-1.21.1-j21.txt   md5 a0e1201885684d8fc077e30f34dc52d2

## axis 3 is zero, and that is stage 1's result finally being measured

The objective's staged line recorded axis3 at 187 BEFORE stage 1. It is 0 now. Stage 1's transfer bridge
and :remapTransferApis did what they claimed; nothing in this port had ever produced a transcript that
could show it, because the earlier numbers came from runs that did not satisfy the M2 predicate (see
"the four things that produce a wrong number rather than an error" in measure.sh's header).

So 161 -> 180 is not a regression. It is the difference between a number taken with a stale energy jar
and a correct classpath, and a number taken with both. The two are not comparable and the earlier figure
should not be quoted again.

## Two bugs had to be cleared to get here, and both were of the same shape

1. BASH WAS NOT ON PATH. `measure.sh` invokes `bash` by name at three points. PowerShell has no `bash`;
   the interpreter here is `C:\Program Files\Git\bin\bash.exe`. Gradle's own configure phase already
   appends that directory to PATH for the gate suites, which is why `gradlew` worked and the script did
   not.

2. THE ENERGY JAR'S NAME IS COMPUTED TWICE. `measure.sh` checks `team.reborn.energy.api.EnergyStorage`
   resolves, and on failure prints "is $ENERGY_JAR actually retargeted?". It was not a retarget failure:
   the classpath entry `dumpRuntimeCp` emitted was

       build/energy-api/1.21.1/energy-5.0.0-j21-j21.jar

   while the task actually writes

       build/energy-api/1.21.1/energy-5.0.0-j21.jar

   The `-j21` suffix is applied to a name that already carries it. The diagnostic text points at the
   wrong cause — the file does not exist, so nothing is unreadable about it. This is the third time in
   this port that a script's precondition assertion was right while its suggested remedy was wrong (the
   other two: `:remapTransferApis` blaming a missing prepare when it needed `-Pforbric.stagedRoot`, and
   the suite's `stagedRoot` default).

   Worked around, not fixed, by copying `energy-5.0.0-j21.jar` to `energy-5.0.0-j21-j21.jar`. The real
   fix is to stop appending the suffix when the name already ends with it, in whichever of the two places
   computes it — and they are two places, which is the actual defect.

## What 180 now localises

    axis2-forge-neoforge   91   the largest block, and it maps onto the named absent types:
                                KernelFmlTransformerView 7, KernelNeoTooltips 4,
                                KernelForgePipRenderers 2, KernelForgeOverlayLayers 1,
                                plus the Result/BreakBlockEvent sites whose TSV rows were just fixed
    axis1-minecraft        73   vanilla name differences
    cascades               16   down from 118 in the pre-stage-1 accounting
    axis3                   0

The 22 absent types from stages 19-20 are the 91, not the 180. That is the first time the absent-type
count and the error count have been put on the same denominator, and it is why the "compile out 13
subsystems" decision is worth about half the total rather than most of it.

## Not verified

Nothing was launched. No jar was produced. The suite baseline was taken separately and reads
5,635 / 78 / 0 / 846 on this machine — 76 TempDir IOExceptions plus exactly the two known real failures,
which is the self-consistent reading and NOT the "79 / 842" the objective quotes. That 79 has never been
reproduced here.

# Stage 22: the reproducible measurement loop, and what it now says

After stage 21 the loop is: change one thing, run measure.sh, read TOTAL and the four axes. Three
consecutive changes have gone through it and all three passed the objective's stage-2 acceptance line
(axis 2 down, axis 1 and axis 3 byte-identical, cascades not up):

    commit      TOTAL  axis1  axis2  axis3  cascades   transcript md5
    (baseline)    180     73     91      0        16     a0e1201885...
    51d515d       165     73     84      0         8     ddc1e5b209...
    872337f       162     73     83      0         6     64b2d3a3fa...

Running from the restored baseline that is 180 -> 162 with axis 1 pinned at 73 the whole way, which is the
evidence that nothing was moved between buckets: axis 1 is vanilla-name work and these three commits
touched only MinecraftForge/NeoForge spellings.

## The $ lesson, stated once

Both 70ee427 and 370cbf5 wrote a rename target as `Type$Nested`. javap prints that because javap reads
bytecode. A rename table's target column is JAVA SOURCE TEXT and must be dotted. A whole-table scan -- 48
rules, 0 with $ in the source column and (after 872337f) 0 in the target column -- is what backs the claim
that it is now clean, not a search for suspects.

## What the remaining 162 actually are

Measured by counting the transcript's `symbol:` and `package ... does not exist` lines, and grouping by
the stage-19/20 absent-type list:

    ~62  zero-substrate, decided already:
         PictureInPictureRenderer 14, PictureInPictureRenderState 8,
         PictureInPictureRendererRegistration 4, pip packages 3            = 29  pip
         TooltipDisplay 8, ItemTooltipHandler 5, TooltipLocation 2,
         TooltipAppender 2, tooltip packages 3, RegisterTooltipAppendersEvent 2 = 22  tooltips
         FuelValues 9 + 5 packages                                        = 14  (26.x-only API)
         TransformingClassLoader 6, FMLMixinClassProcessor 4,
         ClassProcessorSet 3, transformation packages 6                     = 19  class transformation
         Hud 5                                                             =  5  the 26.x Gui/Hud split
    ~9   the rest of the 22 named types (RecipeContentPayload 4, BUS 3,
         ListenerKey 2, ForgeLayer 2 -- the latter two also descriptor-bound)
    ~15  not in that list and worth looking at individually:
         getKeyEvent() 5, FluidModel 4, TypedInstance 2, ValueInput 2, Weighted 2
    6    cascades

So "compile out the 13 zero-substrate subsystems" is worth about 62 of 162, i.e. 38% -- and the other 100
are NOT that decision. That is the number the A/B/C question should have been asked with and was not.

## Two concrete next fixes, with the evidence already gathered

`getKeyEvent()` x5, all in KernelGameClientEvents. 1.21.1's InputEvent$Key has

    private final int key, scanCode, action, modifiers;
    public int getKey();

and NO getKeyEvent(). 26.2's first argument to ForgeHooksClient.onKeyInput is an event object; 1.21.1's is
an int. So this is a signature change, not a rename, and the fix is an OVERLAY of
KernelGameClientEvents.java -- not a table row, because src/runtime/java must keep compiling for 26.2.

`new BlockEvent.BreakEvent(level, pos, state, player, result)` in KernelGameBlockEvents:279. 1.21.1's
constructor is `(Level, BlockPos, BlockState, Player)` -- four arguments. The five-argument form is the
cascade that appeared once BreakBlockEvent started resolving at all, which is a good illustration of why
cascades must not be "fixed" directly: the root fix is what created it.

Both need an overlay and one measurement each. At ~15 minutes per measurement that is the binding
constraint, not the code.

## Not verified

No jar, no launch. 162 is a compile-error count under M2. The suite baseline on this machine reads
5,635 / 78 / 0 / 846 (76 TempDir + the two known real ones); the "79 / 842" in the objective has never
been reproduced here.
---
# Stage 23: OPEN -- eight rules are orphaned, and that contradicts the progress line

This stage records a contradiction rather than a result, because I could not resolve it before running out
of room, and leaving it claimed as a success would be worse than leaving it open.

## What was observed

Attempting the BreakEvent overlay (below) made `:normalizeRuntimeJava` run its `doLast` guard for the first
time in this session. It reported:

    1.21.1.tsv: 8 rule(s) matched nothing in src/runtime/java:
      net.minecraftforge.event.entity.player.PlayerInteractEvent.RightClickBlock.BUS
      net.minecraftforge.event.entity.player.PlayerInteractEvent.RightClickItem.BUS
      net.minecraftforge.event.entity.player.PlayerInteractEvent.LeftClickBlock.BUS
      net.minecraftforge.event.level.BlockEvent.EntityPlaceEvent.BUS
      net.neoforged.neoforge.event.level.block.BreakBlockEvent
      net.minecraft.util.TriState
      BlockEvent.BreakEvent.BUS
      BreakBlockEvent

## Why that is a contradiction

`BreakBlockEvent` does appear in `src/runtime/java`: `KernelGameBlockEvents` subscribes with
`BreakBlockEvent.class` and declares `static boolean fireBreak(BreakBlockEvent neoEvent)`. So "matched
nothing" is false for that name as written.

Yet TOTAL moved 180 -> 165 -> 162 across the two commits that rewrote those rules' targets, and the axes
behaved exactly as a real fix would (axis 2 down, axis 1 and axis 3 unmoved). If the rules had never
fired, nothing should have moved.

Both statements cannot hold. One of these is true and I have not established which:

  a) the guard skips overlaid files, and `KernelGameBlockEvents` was skipped in every previous run because
     it had no overlay -- so "matched nothing" is a statement about the NON-overlaid subset, and adding an
     overlay is what removed the file from that subset and orphaned the rules. Under this reading the
     180 -> 165 -> 162 improvement is real and the guard's message is misleading.
  b) some of those eight never matched and the improvement came from fewer rules than claimed. Under this
     reading the progress line is partly mis-attributed.

The `doFirst` block at build.gradle:467-475 already implements exactly this skip for the overlay check, so
(a) has a mechanism behind it. But the guard at :510-517 is in `doLast` and I have not read how it collects
its input set. That read is the next step.

## The overlay content, which is believed correct and is NOT committed

    BlockEvent.BreakEvent forgeEvent = new BlockEvent.BreakEvent(level, neoEvent.getPos(),
            neoEvent.getState(), neoEvent.getPlayer());
    forgeEvent.setResult(seed(neoEvent.isCanceled()));
    return vetoed(forgeEvent);

replacing

    return vetoed(new BlockEvent.BreakEvent(level, neoEvent.getPos(), neoEvent.getState(), neoEvent.getPlayer(),
            seed(neoEvent.isCanceled())));

Evidence for it: 1.21.1's `BlockEvent$BreakEvent` has exactly one constructor, `(Level, BlockPos,
BlockState, Player)` -- javap, four arguments, no Result. `Event.setResult(net.minecraftforge.eventbus.
api.Event$Result)` exists -- javap. `seed()` returns `Result`, which the stage-22 table row maps to
`Event.Result`. Setting after construction is equivalent to setting during it.

It was written to `src/runtime/mc-line/1.21.1/java/.../KernelGameBlockEvents.java`, made the guard fire,
and was deleted to restore a measurable tree. The tree at HEAD is clean and measures 162.

## State at HEAD

    TOTAL 162   axis1 73   axis2 83   axis3 0   cascades 6
    transcript md5 64b2d3a3fa7ff23315e62564c4714f65   (identical before and after the overlay attempt)

## The hazard this exposes, independent of which reading is right

`normalizeRuntimeJava`'s "a rule matched nothing" guard lives in `doLast`. Gradle's up-to-date check means
`doLast` does not run when the task is UP-TO-DATE, and a table edit plus a source tree that has not changed
in the same way leaves it UP-TO-DATE. So the guard's guarantee -- "every rule in the table fires" -- holds
on the first build after a table change and need not hold afterwards. Anyone who edits 1.21.1.tsv and then
measures may be measuring a table whose rows silently do nothing. That is the fifth way this project has
found for a number to be wrong without anything erroring, and it is the first one that lives in Gradle's
incremental machinery rather than in a hand-run command.
